A spectral ansatz for the long-time homogenization of the wave equation
Mitia Duerinckx, Antoine Gloria, Matthias Ruf

Abstract
. Consider the wave equation with heterogeneous coefficients in the homogenization regime. At large times, the wave interacts in a nontrivial way with the heterogeneities, giving rise to effective dispersive effects. The main achievement of the present work is a new ansatz for the long-time two-scale expansion inspired by spectral analysis. Based on this spectral ansatz, we extend and refine all previous results in the field, proving homogenization up to optimal timescales with optimal error estimates, and covering all the standard assumptions on heterogeneities (both periodic and stationary random settings).



1.
Introduction

1.1.
General overview

Let
d\geq 1
be the space dimension and let
\boldsymbol{a}
be a symmetric coefficient field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies the boundedness and ellipticity properties

(1.1)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{a}(x)\xi|\leq|\xi|,\qquad\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(x)\xi\geq\lambda|\xi|^{2},\qquad\text{for all $x,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$},

for some
\lambda>0
.
We shall consider both the case when
\boldsymbol{a}
is periodic and the case when
\boldsymbol{a}
is a stationary ergodic random field (in the latter case, we restrict to a Gaussian model for illustration, cf. Definition
1.3
).
Given an impulse

f\in C^{\infty}_{c}((0,\infty);\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
we consider the ancient solution of the associated linear wave equation

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla)u_{\varepsilon}=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
u_{\varepsilon}=f=0,&\text{for $t<0$,}\end{array}\right.

in the homogenization regime
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, and we are interested in the accurate description of the long-time behavior of the flow. The reason why we focus on ancient solutions (with
u_{\varepsilon}=f=0
for
t<0
) is to ensure the well-preparedness of the wave and to avoid propagating time oscillations; see the discussion in Section
1.6
.
Note that we can also consider the case of strongly elliptic systems up to obvious modifications. It is however crucial that coefficients be symmetric (to avoid exponentially growing modes in the Floquet-Bloch theory).

On short timescales
t=O(1)
, standard theory
[
7
]
ensures that the flow can be approximated to leading order by the ancient solution of a homogenized wave equation,

(1.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla)\bar{u}=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
\bar{u}=f=0,&\text{for $t<0$,}\end{array}\right.

where the (constant) effective coefficient
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}
is the same as for the homogenization of the corresponding steady-state problem. This means that homogenization and time evolution decouple to leading order on short timescales.
As first understood by Santosa and Symes
[
25
]
, this is however no longer the case on longer timescales: more precisely, a non-trivial interaction between homogenization and time evolution appears as soon as
t\geq O(\varepsilon^{-2})
in the periodic setting,
leading to a dispersive correction to the naïvely homogenized wave equation (
1.3
).

In the periodic setting, the first rigorous analysis of this phenomenon is based on spectral theory, more specifically on Floquet–Bloch theory, and is due to Lamacz
[
23
]
in one space dimension, and to Dohmal, Lamacz, and Schweizer
[
9
,
10
]

in higher dimension.
They proved the convergence to some suitable dispersive homogenized wave equation up to times
t\ll\varepsilon^{-3}
.
Due to the use of the Floquet–Bloch theory, it was not clear that this approach could be applied beyond the periodic setting to other standard frameworks for homogenization (such as quasi-periodic or random coefficient fields). To treat such cases, Benoit and the second author developed in
[
5
]
an approximate version of the Floquet–Bloch theory, which was inspired by
[
2
]
and by the observation that the derivative of the Bloch wave with respect to the wave number at 0 is a multiple of the standard corrector in homogenization. Extending this to all orders,
[
5
]
introduced a notion of ‘Taylor–Bloch waves’, which approximately diagonalize the elliptic operator
-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla
at low wavenumber. In contrast to the standard Floquet–Bloch analysis, this approximate spectral approach is easily transferred to the random setting as it does not rely on the existence of exact Bloch waves (see
[
14
]
for an extension of these ideas to other regimes).
In the periodic case, this allowed the authors of
[
5
]
to derive a whole hierarchy of higher-order homogenized equations that are valid to leading order up to times
t\leq O(\varepsilon^{-\ell})
for any
\ell\geq 0
.
These higher-order homogenized equations are well-posed up to truncating high-frequencies.
In the random case, they also managed to cover the case of random coefficient fields, for which a homogenized description can only be found up to some maximal timescale
t=O(\varepsilon^{-\ell_{*}})
.
Although this analysis allows reaching long timescales, it does not provide approximations with optimal accuracy. There is indeed a strong limitation in the analysis: since the impulse
f
in equation (
1.2
) is not adapted to
O(\varepsilon)
oscillations of the coefficients, Bloch waves at low wave number only describe the solution to leading order, and Bloch waves at higher wave number should further be taken into account for a finer description. The main difficulty is that Bloch waves at higher wave number are not easily related to “correctors” in homogenization, so that it was unclear how to improve the accuracy in
[
5
]
.

Shortly after
[
5
]
, following a variant of classical two-scale expansion methods
[
6
]
, Allaire, Lamacz, and Rauch
[
3
]
and Abdulle and Pouchon
[
24
,
1
]

obtained similar results in the periodic setting and did improve the accuracy in the description of the wave flow on long timescales in terms of some two-scale expansion.
Interestingly, and as opposed to the equations obtained by approximate spectral theory, the homogenized equations obtained in
[
3
,
24
,
1
]
have to be significantly reformulated several times before they give rise to a well-posed problem (this is called the “criminal approximation” in
[
3
]
). Because the number of such reformulations increases with the order of accuracy, and although arbitrarily long times and optimal accuracy can be reached,
the “infinite-order” homogenized operator they implicitly define cannot be inverted even for very smooth functions.

On the one hand, approaches inspired by spectral theory are physically-motivated (for waves equations, the spectrum is of the essence), yield well-posed equations valid for long times, but so far were limited in terms of accuracy. On the other hand, approaches based on systematic two-scale expansions allow reaching both long times and optimal accuracy, but essentially require as many reformulations as the order of accuracy to obtain a well-posed equation, which prevents one from inverting the associated “infinite-order” homogenized operator (in other words, the bound on the homogenization error is not sharp).
To sum up, a physically-motivated two-scale expansion to reach long times and optimal accuracy was still missing.

The main aim of this paper is to introduce a full spectral two-scale expansion for (
1.2
), that extends
[
5
]
to any order and allows us to invert the “infinite-order” homogenized operator for smooth enough impulses
f
.
This spectral two-scale expansion is defined in Theorem
1
, whereas the infinite-order result is given in Corollary
1
. This infinite-order result shows that the analysis we do here is indeed paying off (it cannot be obtained using the systematic two-scale expansions of
[
3
,
24
,
1
]
).
The main insight of this work is encapsulated in Proposition
1.5
, which reformulates the
explicit formula for the solution of (
1.2
) based on Floquet–Bloch theory in a way that leverages an intrinsic two-scale expansion (which we call
spectral
two-scale expansion).
A fundamental physical feature of this spectral two-scale expansion is the following: corrections due to the fact that the impulse
f
is not adapted to
O(\varepsilon)
oscillations of the coefficients
are local with respect to
f
,
see Remark
1.1
. This original feature should be of interest to the engineering community, as it means in particular that the expansion actually reduces (essentially) to the much simpler one used in
[
5
]
outside the support of the impulse.
The main merit of this work is to work out this spectral two-scale expansion and its combinatorial structure.
The adaptation from the periodic to the random setting is essentially routine to the expert in stochastic homogenization.
It is however important and shows the limitation of homogenization techniques for waves in random media — which is why a detailed statement is included in Theorem
3
.

Last,
we also thoroughly discuss the two-scale approach of
[
3
,
24
,
1
]
(which we call
geometric
two-scale expansion, cf. Section
1.5
), and we relate it to the new spectral two-scale expansion. This essentially amounts to comparing
redundant hierarchies of corrector equations, which we do in an algorithmic way. As an output, we
improve the error analysis of
[
3
,
24
,
1
]
, cf. (
1.12
).

The rest of this introduction is organized as follows: In Section
1.2
, we state our main results on long-time homogenization, both in the periodic and in the random settings, comparing the results obtained with the spectral and the geometric approaches.
Next, in Section
1.3
, we comment on the important question of the well-posedness of the formal homogenized equations (cf. (
1.5
) and (
1.10
) below). In Sections
1.4
and
1.5
, we motivate the special form of the spectral and the geometric two-scale expansions. We conclude in Section
1.6
with a discussion of the well-preparedness assumption for (
1.2
), going beyond the framework of ancient solutions.

Notation

\bullet

We write
\nabla=(\nabla_{j})_{1\leq j\leq d}
for the gradient with respect to the space variable,
\partial_{t}
for the time derivative, and
(D_{j})_{0\leq j\leq d}
for the space-time gradient with
D_{0}=\partial_{t}
and
D_{j}=\nabla_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq d
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\nabla^{n}=(\nabla^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}})_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\leq d}
the
n
th-order spatial derivative.

\bullet

For a vector field
F
and a matrix field
G
, we set
(\nabla F)_{jl}=\nabla_{l}F_{j}
and
(\nabla\cdot G)_{j}=\nabla_{l}G_{jl}
(we systematically use Einstein’s summation convention for repeated indices). We also denote by
(\nabla F)^{T}
the pointwise transposed field,
(\nabla F)^{T}_{jl}=(\nabla F)_{lj}
.

\bullet

Given to matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we denote by
A:B
their inner product defined by
A:B=A_{ij}B_{ij}
(again using Einstein’s summation convention).
For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and an
n
th-order tensor
T=(T_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}})_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\leq d}
, we use the notation
T\odot\xi^{\otimes n}=T_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}\xi_{j_{1}}\ldots\xi_{j_{n}}
for the contraction. For two symmetric tensors
T=(T_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}
and
S=(S_{j_{1}\ldots j_{m}})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}
, we define their symmetric tensor product
T\otimes_{s}S
as the
(n+m)
th-order symmetric tensor characterized by
(T\otimes_{s}S)\odot\xi^{\otimes(n+m)}=(T\odot\xi^{\otimes n})(S\odot\xi^{\otimes m})
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\bullet

The spatial Fourier transform of a function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\hat{f}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\xi\cdot x}u(x)\,dx
, and the inverse Fourier transform by
f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\hat{f}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi
with
d^{*}\xi=(2\pi)^{-d}d\xi
.

\bullet

We set
\langle s\rangle:=(1+|s|^{2})^{1/2}
, and we similarly define the pseudo-differential operator
\langle\nabla\rangle
with Fourier symbol
(1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. More generally, given a continuous map
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the pseudo-differential operator
\chi(\nabla)
with Fourier-symbol
\chi(\xi)
. Moreover, given an
n
th-order tensor
T=(T_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}})_{1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\leq d}
we define the differential operator
T\odot\nabla^{n}=T_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}\nabla^{n}_{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}
.

\bullet

\mathbb{N}
stands for the set of nonnegative integers. For a multi-index
n=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we let
|n|=n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}
.

\bullet

\mathbb{E}\left[\cdot\right]
stands for expectation in the random setting, and is also used in the periodic setting as a short-hand notation for averaging on the unit cell
Q=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{d}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=\fint_{Q}X
.

\bullet

We denote by
C\geq 1
any constant that only depends on the dimension
d
and on the ellipticity constant
\lambda
in (
1.1
). We use the notation
\lesssim
(resp.
\gtrsim
) for
\leq C\times
(resp.
\geq\frac{1}{C}\times
) up to such a multiplicative constant
C
. We write
\ll
(resp.
\gg
) for
\leq\frac{1}{C}\times
(resp.
\geq C\times
) up to a sufficiently large multiplicative constant
C
. We add subscripts to indicate dependence on other parameters. We use Landau’s big-
O
notation in a less rigorous way to indicate the scaling behavior of quantities, where the precise bounds can depend on many parameters.

\bullet

The ball centered at
x
of radius
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
B_{r}(x)
, and we set
B(x)=B_{1}(x)
,
B_{r}=B_{r}(0)
, and
B=B_{1}(0)
.

\bullet

When defining hierarchies of correctors and of homogenized coefficients, we take the convention that all quantities that are not defined are implicitly set to zero: e.g.
\psi^{n}=0
for
n<0
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}=0
for
n\leq 0
in Definition
2.1
, etc.

1.2.
Main results: long-time homogenization

Our main results yield long-time error estimates for the two-scale expansion of the heterogeneous wave
equation (
1.2
) with optimal accuracy up to the optimal maximal timescale, with optimal norms (see below the statements for a precise discussion of optimality). We separately consider the periodic and the random setting
s
; the case of quasi-periodic coefficient fields could be treated as well but is skipped for shortness.

1.2.1.
Periodic setting

We start with the case when the coefficient field
\boldsymbol{a}
is periodic on the unit cell
Q=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{d}
. The main result of this contribution provides a two-scale expansion with optimal error estimate up to times
t\leq O(\varepsilon^{-\ell})
for any
\ell\geq 0
.
This is obtained by extending the spectral approach of
[
5
]
to higher-order accuracy in form of a suitable two-scale expansion: while the error estimate in
[
5
]
saturated at
O(\varepsilon)
, we now reach accuracy
O(\varepsilon^{\ell}t)
, cf. (
1.6
).
The formal homogenized equation (
1.5
) takes the form of a dispersive correction of (
1.3
) and the discussion of its well-posedness is postponed to Section
1.3
; note that the homogenized differential operator in (
1.5
) below is necessarily symmetric in the sense that
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}=0
for all
k
even.
1
1

1

This is a consequence of the fact this operator appears as the homogenization of the self-adjoint wave operator; see
[
5
, Proposition 1]
or our direct proof of Proposition
3.5
below.

The proof of this main result is displayed in Section
2
, together with the definition of spectral correctors.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be
Q
-periodic.
There exist sequences of spectral correctors
\{\psi^{n}\}_{n}
and
\{\zeta^{n,m}\}_{n,m}

obtained as solutions of elliptic problems on the periodic cell
Q
, a sequence of homogenized tensors
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\}_{n}
, and a sequence of Fourier multiplier
\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n}
with
|\gamma_{n}(\xi)|\leq 1
, cf. Definition
2.1
, such that the following holds.
For all
\ell\geq 1
and for any impulse
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
f=0
for
t<0
, the ancient solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
)
is accurately described by the ‘spectral two-scale expansion’

(1.4)

S_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell},f]\,:=\,\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\varepsilon^{n}\psi^{n}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\gamma_{\ell}(\varepsilon\nabla)\nabla^{n}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}\\
+\varepsilon^{3}\sum_{2m=0}^{\ell-3}(-1)^{m}\varepsilon^{2m}\sum_{n=0}^{\ell-3-2m}\varepsilon^{n}\zeta^{n,m}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\gamma_{\ell}(\varepsilon\nabla)\nabla^{n+1}\partial_{t}^{2m}f,

where
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
is an ancient solution of the following formal homogenized equation on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, in one of the meanings provided in Lemma
2.10
,

(1.5)

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{k-1}\Big)\nabla\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}=f+O(\varepsilon^{\ell}).

(Here,
\psi^{n}
is an
n
th-order tensor field,
\zeta^{n,m}
is an
(n+1)
th-order tensor field, and
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}
is a matrix-valued
(k-1)
th-order tensor — see the notation section for the contraction
\odot
of tensors of the same order.)
More precisely, we have the following error estimate: for all
\ell\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t},f^{t}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t},f^{t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\,\leq\,(\varepsilon C)^{\ell}\langle t\rangle\,\|\langle D\rangle^{C\ell}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}((0,t);\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}.\qed

Remark 1.1
.

The spectral two-scale expansion (
1.4
) has an important property of physical interest: the second sum contains a series of terms that are all local with respect to the impulse
f
, and this local contribution vanishes outside the support of
f
.
This specific form for the expansion owes to the Bloch wave analysis of Section
1.4
.
It also illustrates the superiority of the present analysis over
[
5
]
: although outside the support of the impulse the expansion in
[
5
]
has essentially the same form as (
1.4
) (up to the pseudo-differential operator
\gamma_{\ell}(\varepsilon\nabla)
), it does not reach accuracy beyond the order
O(\varepsilon)
.
∎

Remark 1.2
.

The above two-scale expansion (
1.4
) involves the pseudo-differential operator
\gamma_{\ell}(\varepsilon\nabla)
. Although convenient in the analysis, it might not be so in practice (e.g. for numerical purposes).
As shown in (
2.6
), we can expand
\gamma_{\ell}(\varepsilon\nabla)=1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\gamma_{\ell}^{k}\odot\nabla^{k}
for some explicit tensors
\{\gamma_{\ell}^{k}\}_{k}
, so that for the purpose of (
1.6
) it can be approximated to the required accuracy
O(\varepsilon^{\ell})
by a finite-order differential operator. Note that the pseudo-differential operator has some intrinsic spectral interpretation, playing the role of a normalization of Bloch waves, cf. Section
1.4
.
∎

The scaling
\varepsilon^{\ell}\langle t\rangle
of the error (
1.6
) is optimal.
This can be seen in this periodic setting on the explicit spectral formula (
1.25
) for the solution (with a first order Taylor expansion of the time integrand) for a forcing term
f
compactly supported in time. The scaling of the error with respect to the norm of
f
involves the optimal order of derivatives wrt to
\ell
(we indeed need at least
\ell
derivatives to define
\bar{u}^{\ell}
– we have not tried to optimize the multiplicative constant
C
). In particular it implies the summability of the two-scale expansion (or the invertibility of the associated “infinite-order homogenized operator” as pointed out in the introduction) in form of the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be
Q
-periodic. Given an impulse
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
that decays as
t\downarrow-\infty
in the sense of
\int_{-\infty}^{0}|t|\|\langle D\rangle^{k}f^{t}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,dt<\infty
for any
k\geq 0
,
consider the unique solution of the associated heterogeneous wave equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla)u_{\varepsilon}=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
{\lim_{t\downarrow-\infty}u_{\varepsilon}^{t}\equiv 0},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$}.\end{array}\right.

Then, in terms of the two-scale expansion (
1.4
), with
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
now denoting the corresponding solution of the formal homogenized equation (
1.5
) in the sense of Lemma
2.10
, we have for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1.7)

\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t},f^{t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\leq\,(\varepsilon C)^{\ell}\int_{-\infty}^{t}(t-s)\|\langle D\rangle^{C\ell}f^{s}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,ds.

In particular, if for instance the impulse takes the form
f^{t}(x)=f_{1}(t)f_{2}(x)
, where
f_{1}
has a smooth and compactly supported Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}
and where
f_{2}
has a compactly supported Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
then the two-scale expansion is summable in the following sense: for
0<\varepsilon\ll_{f}1
small enough (only depending on
d,\lambda,f
), for all
T\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1.8)

\lim_{\ell\uparrow\infty}\,\sup_{t\leq T}\,\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t},f^{t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.\qed

For comparison,
we display the corresponding result that can be obtained instead of Theorem
1
when using a more standard “geometric” approach to devise a two-scale expansion as in
[
3
,
24
,
1
]
(see Section
1.5
for an explanation of the naming “geometric”).
We slightly improve the error estimates of
[
3
]
thanks to the use of suitable flux correctors (see Remark
3.7
). Yet, the scaling with respect to
\ell
in the error estimate (
1.11
) is much worse than the one in Theorem
1
by a factor
\ell^{\ell}
, thus showing the advantage of the spectral approach.
(In particular, Corollary
1
does not follow from Theorem
2
.)
The proof is displayed in Section
3
, together with the definition of hyperbolic correctors.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be
Q
-periodic.
There exists a sequence of hyperbolic correctors
\{\phi^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
obtained as solutions of elliptic problems on the periodic cell
Q
, and there exists a sequence of homogenized coefficients
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
, cf. Definition
3.1
, such that the following holds.
For all
\ell\geq 1
and for any impulse
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
f=0
for
t<0
, the ancient solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
) is accurately described by the ‘hyperbolic two-scale expansion’

(1.9)

H^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}]\,:=\,\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell-n}\varepsilon^{n+m}\phi^{n,m}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\nabla^{n}\partial_{t}^{m}\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell},

where
\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
is an ancient solution of the following formal homogenized equation on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, up to a suitable revamping, cf. (
3.7
), in one of the meanings provided in Lemma
2.10
,

(1.10)

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{v}^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell-n}\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{n-1}(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{m}\Big)\nabla\bar{v}^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,f+O(\varepsilon^{\ell}).

(Here,
\phi^{n,m}
is an
n
th-order tensor and
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}
is a matrix-valued
(n-1)
th-order tensor.)
More precisely, we have the following error estimate: for all
\ell\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-H^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-H^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\,\leq\,(\varepsilon C\ell)^{\ell}\langle t\rangle\,\|\langle D\rangle^{C\ell}\langle\varepsilon CD\rangle^{\ell^{2}}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}((0,t);\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}.\qed

It is instructive to compare spectral and geometric two-scale expansions (
1.4
) and (
1.9
).
Outside the support of
f
, (
1.4
) provides an approximation of
u_{\varepsilon}
to order
O(\varepsilon^{\ell})
by a sum of
\ell+1
terms, whereas (
1.9
) reaches a similar order of approximation with a sum of
\frac{1}{4}(\ell+1)(\ell+4)
terms (note that
\phi^{n,m}
vanishes for
m
odd, cf. Definition
3.1
). This difference between
O(\ell)
and
O(\ell^{2})
terms in the expansions illustrates the more intrinsic character of the spectral two-scale expansion and its superiority in terms of estimates.
There is obviously a link between spectral and hyperbolic correctors, and spectral correctors
\{\psi^{n},\zeta^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
can indeed be recovered as linear combinations of hyperbolic correctors
\{\phi^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
with coefficients that are
nonlinear functions
of hyperbolic homogenized coefficients
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
. Working out the precise algorithmic relation between the spectral and geometric approaches is quite involved and necessarily algorithmic. This is the subject of Section
4
.
In particular, in combination with Theorem
1
, one can improve (
1.11
) a posteriori to

(1.12)

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-H^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-H^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\leq\,(\varepsilon C)^{\ell}\langle t\rangle\,\|\langle D\rangle^{C\ell}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}((0,t);\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))},

thus removing the spurious factor
\ell^{\ell}
.
This constitutes a significant strengthening of the error analysis of
[
3
]
and could not be obtained from the geometric two-scale expansion approach only.

1.2.2.
Random setting

We turn to the case of a stationary ergodic random coefficient field
\boldsymbol{a}
. For simplicity and illustration, we shall focus on the following Gaussian model.

Definition 1.3
.

The coefficient field
\boldsymbol{a}
is said to be
Gaussian with parameter
\beta>0
if it has the form
\boldsymbol{a}=h(G)
for some
h\in\text{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{d\times d}
with
k\geq 1
and for some
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued centered stationary Gaussian random field
G
such that the covariance function

c(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[G(x)\otimes G(0)\right],\qquad c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},

has
\beta
-algebraic decay at infinity,
|c(x)|\,\leq\,(1+|x|)^{-\beta}
.
∎

The analysis of Theorem
1
can be repeated in this Gaussian setting, and leads to the following two-scale expansion result with optimal error estimate up to the optimal maximal timescale. Note that the maximal timescale depends on the decay rate
\beta
for correlations and saturates in case of integrable decay
\beta>d
(which corresponds to the strongest mixing possible in the Gaussian setting and yields the central limit theorem scaling for large-scale averages of the coefficients).
This result extends
[
5
]
to higher-order accuracy.
The proof is displayed in Section
2.8
.
Exactly as in the periodic case above, a corresponding result could also be obtained in terms of the geometric two-scale expansion; we skip the detail for shortness.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be Gaussian with parameter
\beta>0
in the above sense, and define

\ell_{*}:=\lceil\tfrac{\beta\wedge d}{2}\rceil.

We can construct spectral correctors
\{\psi^{n}\}_{n\leq\ell_{*}}
and
\{\zeta^{n,m}\}_{n+2m\leq\ell_{*}-3}
as well-behaved solutions of some hierarchy of elliptic problems on the probability space, homogenized tensors
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\}_{n\leq\ell_{*}}
, and a Fourier multiplier
\gamma_{\ell}
with
|\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)|\leq 1
, cf. Appendix
A
, such that the following holds.
For any impulse
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
f=0
for
t<0
, the ancient solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
)
is accurately described by the corresponding spectral two-scale expansion
S_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell},f]
given in (
1.4
), where
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
is an ancient solution of the corresponding formal homogenized equation (
1.5
) in one of the meanings provided in Lemma
2.10
.
More precisely, we have the following error estimate: for all
t\geq 0
and
q<\infty
,

\|u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell_{*}}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell_{*};t},f^{t}]\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}(\Omega;\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\|D(u_{\varepsilon}^{t}-S^{\ell_{*}}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell_{*};t},f^{t}])\|_{\operatorname{L}^{q}(\Omega;\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\\
\,\lesssim_{q}\,\|\langle\cdot\rangle\langle D\rangle^{C\ell}f\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{2}}|\!\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{1}{2}}&:&\beta>d,\penalty\ \text{$d$ even},\\
\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{2}}\big(\langle t\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)&:&\beta>d,\penalty\ \text{$d$ odd},\\
\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{2}}|\!\log\varepsilon|&:&\beta=d,\penalty\ \text{$d$ even},\\
\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{2}}\big((\langle t\rangle|\!\log\varepsilon|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)&:&\beta=d,\penalty\ \text{$d$ odd},\\
\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{\beta}{2}}|\!\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{1}{2}}&:&\beta<d,\penalty\ \beta\in 2\mathbb{N},\\
\langle t\rangle\varepsilon^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\big(\langle t\rangle^{1-\{\frac{\beta}{2}\}}\wedge\varepsilon^{-\{\frac{\beta}{2}\}}\big)&:&\beta<d,\penalty\ \beta\notin 2\mathbb{N},\end{array}\right.

with the short-hand notation
\{\frac{\beta}{2}\}=\frac{\beta}{2}-\lfloor\frac{\beta}{2}\rfloor\in[0,1)
for the fractional part of
\frac{\beta}{2}
.
∎

For short times
t=O(1)
, in the setting of integrable covariance
\beta>d
, the above two-scale expansion error estimate is
O(\varepsilon^{d/2})
(up to a logarithmic correction in even dimensions).
This is optimal and random fluctuations of
u_{\varepsilon}
become dominant beyond this scaling since fluctuations naturally have the scaling
O(\varepsilon^{d/2})
of the central limit theorem, cf. e.g.
[
13
]
.
For longer times, the two-scale expansion error further depends on whether the dimension is odd or even due to the growth of correctors: For
\beta>d
, the two-scale expansion error remains negligible
\ll 1
, in the sense that the wave can be accurately described in terms of some homogenized equation, only provided that

(1.13)

t\ll\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}&:&d=1,\\
\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{2}}|\!\log\varepsilon|^{-\frac{1}{2}}&:&\text{$d$ even},\\
\varepsilon^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}&:&\text{$d$ odd $>1$}.\end{array}\right.

Up to such timescales, the above result can be used in particular to derive ballistic transport properties of the wave, see
[
5
,
14
]
, as well as to derive spectral information in a suitable low-energy regime, see
[
12
]
.
Although the two-scale expansion cannot be pushed further in general (except in the case of very specific structure of the coefficient field, see e.g. the remark above Corollary 1.3 in
[
12
]
), ballistic transport could hold for longer times (see e.g. the case of matched impedance in
[
26
]
).

Remarks 1.4

(Extensions)
.

\bullet

Nontrivial initial data:

In Theorems
1
and
3
, we focus on well-prepared data, or equivalently, on ancient solutions of the heterogeneous wave equation, cf. (
1.2
).
If we rather consider the initial-value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla)z_{\varepsilon}=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
z_{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=u^{\circ},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
\partial_{t}z_{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=v^{\circ},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\end{array}\right.

with initial data
u^{\circ},v^{\circ}\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
then, because of ill-preparedness, an
O(\varepsilon)
contribution with almost-periodic time oscillations with
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
frequency is expected to appear and to maintain forever: this is formally described in Section
1.6
below and shows that a two-scale description cannot hold beyond accuracy
O(\varepsilon)
.
This issue is naturally by-passed by rather considering the time-averaged solution

z_{\varepsilon,\theta}^{t}(x)\,:=\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta(t-s)\,z_{\varepsilon}^{s}(x)\,ds,\qquad\text{for some $\theta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})$.}

Indeed, setting
t_{0}:=\inf(\operatorname{supp}\theta)
, an integration by parts ensures that
z_{\varepsilon,\theta}
is the ancient solution of

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla)z_{\varepsilon,\theta}=\theta^{\prime}(t)u^{\circ}+\theta(t)v^{\circ}+f_{\theta},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
z_{\varepsilon,\theta}^{t}=0,&\text{for $t<t_{0}$},\end{array}\right.

in terms of the time-averaged impulse
f_{\theta}^{t}(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta(t-s)\,f^{s}(x)\,ds
.
Hence, an effective approximation for the time-averaged solution
z_{\varepsilon,\theta}
is obtained as a direct consequence of Theorems
1
and
3
for ancient solutions.
Another way to solve this issue is to consider oscillating initial data
(u^{\circ}_{\varepsilon},v^{\circ}_{\varepsilon})
in form
of a spectral two-scale expansion.

\bullet

Heterogeneous mass density:

As in
[
3
]
, we may replace
\partial_{t}^{2}
by
\rho(\frac{x}{\varepsilon})\partial_{t}^{2}
in the wave equation (
1.2
). Provided that the weight function
\rho
satisfies the uniform non-degeneracy condition
\frac{1}{C_{0}}\leq\rho(x)\leq C_{0}
for some constant
C_{0}>0
, it does not change much in the analysis once the definitions of correctors are suitably adapted. The necessary changes are transparent and we skip the detail for shortness∎

1.3.
Well-posedness of homogenized equations

We start by discussing the formal homogenized equation (
1.5
) obtained with the spectral approach, where we recall that
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}=0
for all
k
even.
The obstacle to the well-posedness of this equation is the lack of ellipticity of the operator

(1.16)

-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{k-1}\Big)\nabla,

because of dispersive corrections.
Indeed, the next-order homogenized coefficient
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{3}
can be proved to be non-negative, cf.
[
25
]
, so that equation (
1.5
) is ill-posed in general.
This is not new to homogenization, and a similar difficulty occurs in the elliptic setting when studying higher-order two-scale expansions, see e.g.
[
6
,
15
]
. In this case, one typically uses an inductive method, which, for the wave equation, would read as follows:
for
\ell\geq 1
, set
\bar{w}^{\ell}_{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\varepsilon^{k-1}\tilde{w}^{k}
, where
\tilde{w}^{1}
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla)\tilde{w}^{1}=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
\tilde{w}^{1}=f=0,&\text{for $t<0$},\end{array}\right.

and where for
2\leq k\leq\ell
we inductively define
\tilde{w}^{k}
as the unique solution of

(1.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla)\tilde{w}^{k}=\sum_{j=2}^{k}\nabla\cdot(\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{j}\odot\nabla^{j-1})\nabla\tilde{w}^{k+1-j},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$}\\
\tilde{w}^{k}=0,&\text{for $t<0$}.\end{array}\right.

It is easily checked that this
\bar{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
indeed satisfies (
1.5
). However, as originally observed in
[
3
]
(in the similar setting of (
1.10
)), this notion of solution displays an immoderate growth in time, which destroys any hope of using it for an accurate description of (
1.2
) on long timescales. More precisely, the energy norm
\|\nabla\bar{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell;t}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is expected to behave like
O(\langle\varepsilon t\rangle^{\ell-1})
, which would make the approximation
u_{\varepsilon}\sim S_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}[\bar{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell},f]
trivially false on long timescales
t\gg\varepsilon^{-1}
.
This time growth (also called secular growth) appears as a snowball effect as corrector terms in the above hierarchy of equations for
\{\tilde{w}^{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
have the preceding profiles as sources.

Instead of this naïve inductive method, one must look for another way to rearrange equation (
1.5
).
In fact, as
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\geq\lambda\operatorname{Id}
, we note that the Fourier symbol of the operator (
1.16
) remains positive in a fixed Fourier support for
\varepsilon
small enough. Therefore, if the spatial Fourier transform of the impulse
f
is compactly supported (uniformly in time), then for
\varepsilon
small enough the Duhamel formula allows us to define a unique solution that keeps the same compact support in Fourier space at all times.
Without this additional assumption on
f
, the operator (
1.16
) needs to be modified at high frequencies
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
to ensure ellipticity, and there are different ways to proceed. In the following we discuss the three different regularizing terms that we consider in Theorem
1
. The resulting solutions satisfy (
1.5
) up
to an error of the order
O(\varepsilon^{\ell})
. We briefly describe high-frequency filtering, higher-order regularization, and the so-called Boussinesq trick: these three approaches happen to be essentially equivalent up to higher-order
O(\varepsilon^{\ell})
errors, and we refer to Section
2.3
for the details.

(I)

High-frequency filtering:

In
[
3
]
, the authors proposed to use a low-pass truncation, which amounts to filtering out high frequencies of the impulse: equation (
1.5
) is then replaced by

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{(I)},\ell}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{k-1}\Big)\nabla\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\text{(I)},\ell}=\chi(\varepsilon^{\alpha}\nabla)f,

for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
and some
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\chi|_{\frac{1}{2}B}=1
and
\chi|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B}=0
.

(II)

Higher-order regularization:

The alternative method used in
[
5
]
amounts to regularizing the operator (
1.16
) by adding a higher-order positive operator
\kappa_{\ell}(\varepsilon|\nabla|)^{\ell}(-\triangle)
, where the factor
\kappa_{\ell}>0
is chosen for instance as the smallest value that ensures the following uniform positivity,

\qquad\xi\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes(k-1)}+\kappa_{\ell}|\xi|^{\ell}\Big)\xi\,\geq\,\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda|\xi|^{2},\qquad\text{for all $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$.}

(III)

Boussinesq trick:

This last method proceeds by rearranging the ill-posed equation (
1.5
) and is inspired by the standard perturbative procedure to rearrange the ill-posed Boussinesq equation in the theory of water waves, see e.g.
[
8
]
.
This so-called Boussinesq trick was first adapted to the present setting by Lamacz
[
23
]
in one space dimension for
\ell=3
. It was extended in
[
9
]
to higher dimension for
\ell=3
, and further extended to all orders
\ell\geq 3
by Abdulle and Pouchon
[
1
]
.
It is somehow of the same spirit as the higher-order regularization above, but with the additional twist that it further uses the wave equation itself.
This approach is slightly more intrinsic than the previous two ones, but it also has the disadvantage of involving derivatives of the impulse. Let us illustrate the main idea for
\ell=3
. We first choose
\kappa_{3}>0
as the smallest value such that

\xi\cdot(\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{3}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes 2})\xi+\kappa_{3}|\xi|^{2}(\xi\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\xi)\,\geq\,0,\qquad\text{for all $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$.}

We then decompose the operator (
1.16
) as

\qquad-\nabla\cdot\big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{3}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{2}\big)\nabla\\
\,=\,-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}(1-\kappa_{3}\varepsilon^{2}\triangle)+\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{3}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{2}\Big)\nabla-\kappa_{3}\varepsilon^{2}\triangle(\nabla\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla),

and we use that at leading order the equation (
1.5
) yields

\qquad\nabla\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{3}\,=\,\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{3}-f+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

to the effect that one may reformulate (
1.5
) as

\qquad\penalty\ \partial_{t}^{2}(1-\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{3}\triangle)\bar{u}^{\text{(III)},3}_{\varepsilon}\!-\!\nabla\!\cdot\!\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}(1-\kappa_{3}\varepsilon^{2}\triangle)+\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{3}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{2}\Big)\nabla\bar{u}^{\text{(III)},3}_{\varepsilon}=(1-\varepsilon^{2}\kappa_{3}\triangle)f,

up to an error of order
O(\varepsilon^{4})
. By our choice of
\kappa_{3}
, this equation is well-posed.
This method extends to arbitrary order and we refer to Section
2.3
for the details.

Next, we turn to the well-posedness of the corresponding formal homogenized equation (
1.10
) obtained for the geometric two-scale expansion.
While for equation (
1.5
) the difficulty only came from the lack of ellipticity of the spatial differential operator (
1.16
),
the existence theory for equation (
1.10
) is much more delicate as this equation involves higher-order mixed space-time derivatives. Just as for equation (
1.5
), the inductive method (
1.17
) used in the elliptic setting leads to secular growth of the approximate solution and is of no use in the present situation.
Before being able to use high-frequency filtering, higher-order regularization, or the Boussinesq trick, we need to get rid of higher-order mixed space-time derivatives in (
1.10
), which can be done by iteratively using the equation itself (quite in the spirit of the above presentation of the Boussinesq trick for
\ell=3
). This is called the criminal approximation in
[
3
]
. The thorough revisiting of this idea is the object of Section
3.3
: more precisely, if
\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}
solves (
1.10
), then it is shown to satisfy an equation of the form

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{k-1}\Big)\nabla\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\ell}\,=\,f+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\ell-2}\bar{\boldsymbol{c}}^{n}\odot(\varepsilon D)^{n-1}\Big)\nabla f+O(\varepsilon^{\ell}),

where
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\}_{n}
coincides with the spectral homogenized coefficients and where
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{c}}^{n}\}_{n}
is some family of nonlinear combinations of the hyperbolic coefficients
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
; see Lemma
3.8
for a precise statement.
Now the differential operator in the left-hand side of this equation is the same as in (
1.5
): it displays a lack of ellipticity, but the same approach to well-posedness can be repeated, using either high-frequency filtering, higher-order regularization, or the Boussinesq trick. Note that the right-hand side in the above reformulation of the homogenized equation (
1.10
) differs from the homogenized equation (
1.5
) obtained with the spectral approach, but there is no contradiction as the spectral and hyperbolic two-scale expansions also differ: this demonstrates the actual complexity of the link between spectral and hyperbolic correctors; see Section
4
.

1.4.
Spectral approach and two-scale expansion

This section constitutes the main insight of this contribution: the form of the spectral two-scale ansatz (
1.4
),

(1.18)

u_{\varepsilon}\,\sim\,S^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{u}_{\varepsilon},f]\,:=\,\sum_{n\geq 0}\varepsilon^{n}\psi^{n}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\gamma(\varepsilon\nabla)\nabla^{n}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\\
+\varepsilon^{3}\sum_{n,m\geq 0}(-1)^{m}\varepsilon^{n+2m}\zeta^{n,m}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\gamma(\varepsilon\nabla)\nabla^{n+1}\partial_{t}^{2m}f.

For that purpose, we focus on the periodic setting and first proceed to a fine Floquet–Bloch analysis of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
).
Starting point is an application of the Floquet transform, which is known to transform the heterogeneous wave operator
-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla
on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into a family of fibered wave operators on the periodic Bloch space
\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)
.

When embedding the periodic cell into the physical space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there is an indeterminacy related to the choice of the origin (
q\in Q
), which we consider as an additional variable. Averaging over this variable in
Q
allows us to place ourselves in the setting of continuum stationarity – thus unifying the notation with the random setting. (All the upcoming results actually hold for
q
fixed in the periodic setting.)
Hence, as in
[
5
,
14
,
16
]
, we enrich the structure by considering shifts of the periodic coefficient field and by augmenting the physical space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to include such shifts: we define
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times Q))
such that, for all
q\in Q
,
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)
is the ancient solution of the following shifted wave equation,

(1.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon}+q)\nabla\big)\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)=f,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)=f=0,&\text{for $t<0$}.\end{array}\right.

In this augmented setting, the
\varepsilon
-Floquet transform
[
14
,
16
]
of an element
\tilde{w}\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times Q)
is formally defined as

(\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\tilde{w})(q):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\xi\cdot y}\,\tilde{w}(y,q-\tfrac{y}{\varepsilon})\,dy,

which is
Q
-periodic with respect to
q
. The Fourier inversion formula takes on the following guise, cf.
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
,

(1.20)

\tilde{w}(x,q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\,(\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\tilde{w})(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,d^{*}\xi.

This leads to a direct integral decomposition
2
2

2

Or, more precisely, a family of direct integral decompositions parameterized by
\varepsilon
, which changes the way the unit cell
Q
is embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times Q)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{\oplus}\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)\,\mathfrak{e}_{\xi}\,d^{*}\xi

via Fourier modes
\mathfrak{e}_{\xi}(x):=e^{i\xi\cdot x}
.
Under this decomposition, the above wave equation (
1.19
) is equivalent to the following family of wave equations on the unit cell
Q
: for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\nabla+i\varepsilon\xi)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla+i\varepsilon\xi)\big)\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}=\hat{f}(\xi),&\text{in $\mathbb{R}\times Q$},\\
\mathcal{V}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}=\hat{f}(\xi)=0,&\text{for $t<0$},\end{array}\right.

where we recall that
\hat{f}
stands for the spatial Fourier transform of
f
.
Solving this equation by means of Duhamel’s formula and using (
1.20
) to invert the Floquet transform, we get

(1.21)

\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,q)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\bigg(\frac{\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(t-s)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}1\bigg)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\,ds,

with the short-hand notation

(1.22)

\mathcal{L}_{\xi}:=-(\nabla+i\xi)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla+i\xi).

In other words, the solution can be decomposed as a superposition of fibered evolutions on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)
, and, since the force
f
is not oscillating, only the fibered spectral measures associated with the constant function
1
matter.
It remains to evaluate the space-time oscillating factor in formula (
1.21
) and to extract an effective behavior as
\varepsilon\ll 1
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be
Q
-periodic, let the impulse
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfy
f=0
for
t<0
, and assume for simplicity that the spatial Fourier transform
\hat{f}
is compactly supported uniformly in time. For all
\xi
, the self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}
on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)
defined in (
1.22
) has discrete spectrum and we denote its smallest eigenvalue by
\lambda_{\xi}\geq 0
. For
|\xi|\ll 1
, this eigenvalue is simple and we denote by
w_{\xi}
a corresponding normalized eigenfunction.
We then set

(1.23)

\pi_{\xi}1\,:=\,\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{w_{\xi}}\right]w_{\xi},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{\xi}^{\bot}1\,:=\,1-\pi_{\xi}1,

we note that the map
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q):\xi\mapsto(\lambda_{\xi},\pi_{\xi}1)
is analytic for
|\xi|\ll 1
, and that for
|\xi|\lesssim 1
and
\varepsilon\ll 1
it holds that

(1.24)

|\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi}|\,\lesssim\,|\xi|^{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1-1\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)}\,\lesssim\,\varepsilon|\xi|.

With this notation, for
\varepsilon\ll_{f}1
(depending on the Fourier support of
f
), the above formula (
1.21
) for
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
can be expanded as follows: for all
n\geq 1
,

(1.25)

\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,q)\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\,(\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sin\big((t-s)(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,ds\bigg)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+\varepsilon^{3}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{m}\varepsilon^{2m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\,\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\partial_{t}^{2m}\hat{f}^{t}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+O(\varepsilon^{2(n+1)})\,\|\langle\partial_{t}\rangle^{2n+1}\hat{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}\cap\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))},

in terms of

(1.26)

\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}\,:=\,(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{-m-1}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1,

which for
|\xi|\lesssim 1
is analytic with respect to
\varepsilon\ll 1
and satisfies

\|\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)}\lesssim_{m}|\xi|
.
∎

We emphasize the structure of the above expansion (
1.25
).
Since in (
1.21
) only the fibered spectral measures associated with the constant function
w_{0}\equiv 1
(which is the ground state of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
) matter, the main contribution in (
1.25
) is naturally given by the perturbed ground state
w_{\varepsilon\xi}\propto\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1
. However, as the impulse
f
is not oscillating, hence is not adapted to oscillations of the ground state
w_{\varepsilon\xi}
, higher modes also create another non-vanishing contribution. In other words:

—

The first term in (
1.25
) corresponds to the contribution of the ground state of the fibered operators
\{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
and the time dependence is expressed by some effective evolution determined by the fibered ground eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
.

—

The second term in (
1.25
) is only of order
O(\varepsilon^{3})
and is induced by higher modes. More precisely, the ill-preparedness of the impulse
f
creates a non-trivial oscillatory contribution in Duhamel’s formula due to higher modes, which amounts after time integration to a contribution that is local with respect to
f
.
In particular, note that this term vanishes outside the support of
f
.

For comparison, the Bloch wave approach in
[
23
,
9
,
10
,
5
]
rather focused on the first term in (
1.25
), thus neglecting the
O(\varepsilon^{3})
contribution of higher modes, and further replaced the oscillating factor
\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1
by a simpler (not normalized) proxy that is easier to characterize but that yields an additional
O(\varepsilon)
error.

Proof of Proposition
1.5
.

To evaluate the space-time oscillating factor in (
1.21
), we must investigate the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}
in the perturbative regime
|\xi|\ll 1
. As this self-adjoint operator has compact resolvent by Rellich’s theorem, it has discrete spectrum.
We denote by
\lambda_{\xi}
its smallest eigenvalue, which is nonnegative as
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}
is.
Note that for
\xi=0
the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is
\lambda_{0}=0
and is simple (with constant eigenfunction).
Since the perturbation
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{0}
is
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-bounded with relative norm
\lesssim 1+|\xi|^{2}
, standard perturbation theory
[
22
]
together with the discreteness of the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
ensures that the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\xi}
remains simple for
|\xi|\ll 1
small enough. Moreover, the branch of eigenvalues
\xi\mapsto\lambda_{\xi}
is analytic for
|\xi|\ll 1
, and there is a corresponding analytic branch of eigenfunctions.
Recall the definition of the corresponding projectors
\pi_{\xi},\pi_{\xi}^{\bot}
in the statement, and note that
\pi_{0}1=1
, so that (
1.24
) follows.
Now expanding the constant function
1
with respect to those projectors, identity (
1.21
) turns into

(1.27)

\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{t}(x,q)\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}(\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sin\big((t-s)(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,ds\bigg)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\bigg(\frac{\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(t-s)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1\bigg)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\,ds.

The first right-hand side term is already of the desired form, cf. (
1.25
).
We turn to the second term, which captures the contribution of higher modes.
Due to the discreteness of the spectrum, for
|\xi|\ll 1
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}
has a spectral gap
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}|_{\Im\pi_{\xi}^{\bot}}\,\gtrsim\,1
. Combined with (
1.24
), this yields the following bound for (
1.26
),

\|\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)}\lesssim_{m}|\xi|.

For
n\geq 1
, noting that iterated integration by parts in the time integral yields for all
\lambda>0
,

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(t-s)\lambda^{1/2}\big)}{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1/2}}\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,ds\,=\,\sum_{m=0}^{n}\big(\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda}\big)^{m+1}(-\partial_{t}^{2})^{m}\hat{f}^{t}(\xi)\\
-\big(\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda}\big)^{n+1}\int_{0}^{t}\cos\big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(t-s)\lambda^{1/2}\big)\partial_{s}(-\partial_{s}^{2})^{n}\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,ds,

where we used the vanishing assumption for the impulse at initial time
s=0
,
it then follows from functional calculus that

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\bigg(\frac{\sin\big(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(t-s)(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1\bigg)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\hat{f}^{s}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\,ds\\
\,=\,\varepsilon^{3}\sum_{{m}=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{m}\varepsilon^{2{m}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\cdot x}\,\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{{m}}(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\partial_{t}^{2{m}}\hat{f}^{t}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+O(\varepsilon^{2(n+1)})\,\|\langle\partial_{t}\rangle^{2n+1}\hat{f}\|_{\operatorname{L}^{1}\cap\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}.

Inserting this into (
1.27
) yields the conclusion (
1.25
).
∎

We turn to the applicability of this spectral computation beyond the periodic setting.
In case of a stationary ergodic random coefficient field
\boldsymbol{a}
, a similar Floquet decomposition (
1.21
) can be justified, cf.
[
14
,
16
]
, but the corresponding fibered operators
\{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
are then defined on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega)
, where
\Omega
is the underlying probability space, and typically have non-discrete spectrum. For instance, the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla
on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\Omega)
is expected to be made of a simple eigenvalue at
0
embedded at the bottom of an absolutely continuous spectrum, cf.
[
16
]
.
In this setting, the Floquet–Bloch theory fails and the above perturbative spectral computation cannot be adapted.
As shown in
[
5
,
14
]
, however, an ‘approximate spectral theory’ can be developed: formal Rayleigh–Schrödinger series can be approximately constructed up to a certain accuracy, leading to approximate Bloch waves that can be used to approximately diagonalize the heterogeneous wave operator and describe the flow on long timescales.
Equivalently, we may start from a two-scale ansatz given by the formal
\varepsilon
-expansion of the spectral formula (
1.25
), and then use PDE techniques to show that it indeed provides a good approximation of the flow to a certain accuracy. This approach is the one we use for the proof of Theorems
1
and
3
: it allows us to forget about the underlying spectral interpretation, which is only used to devise an educated guess for a two-scale ansatz.

Finally, let us describe the
\varepsilon
-expansion of the spectral formula (
1.25
), showing that it takes the form of the spectral two-scale ansatz (
1.18
), and let us derive the relevant hierarchy of PDEs for its coefficients. For that purpose, following
[
5
]
, we first consider the (not normalized) ground state
\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}\right]=1
.
Expanding formally

(1.28)

\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}\sim\sum_{n\geq 0}\varepsilon^{n}\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi},\qquad\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi}\sim\sum_{n\geq 1}\varepsilon^{n}\check{\lambda}^{n}_{\xi},

and separating powers of
\varepsilon
in the eigenvalue relation
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}=\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi}\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}
, we find that the maps
\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}:Q\to\mathbb{C}
and coefficients
\check{\lambda}^{n}_{\xi}\in\mathbb{C}
are defined inductively by
\check{\psi}^{0}_{\xi}=1
,
\check{\lambda}^{0}_{\xi}=0
, and for all
n\geq 1
,

(1.29)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}=\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi})+i\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi}+i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-2}_{\xi})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\check{\lambda}^{k}_{\xi}\check{\psi}^{n-k}_{\xi},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}\right]=0,\qquad\check{\lambda}^{n}_{\xi}=-\mathbb{E}\left[i\xi\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}\nabla\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi}+\boldsymbol{a}i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-2}_{\xi})\right].

(Recall that by convention we implicitly set
\check{\psi}_{\xi}^{n}\equiv 0
for
n<0
.)
This hierarchy of equations uniquely defines
\{\check{\psi}_{\xi}^{n},\check{\lambda}_{\xi}^{n}\}_{n}
by the Fredholm alternative since by induction the periodic average of the right-hand side of (
1.29
) vanishes; cf. Section
2.1
. Note that we find
\check{\lambda}^{1}_{\xi}=0
in agreement with (
1.24
).
Next, we normalize
\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}
to get a normalized ground state
w_{\varepsilon\xi}
and we define the corresponding projections
\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}
, cf. (
1.23
),

(1.30)

w_{\varepsilon\xi}=\frac{\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]^{1/2}},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{w_{\varepsilon\xi}}\right]=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]^{1/2}},\qquad\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1=\frac{\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]},

and, in terms of (
1.28
),

(1.31)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\pi^{\bot}_{\varepsilon\xi}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]}\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(-\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}+\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]}\sum_{n\geq 0}\varepsilon^{n}\Big(-\check{\psi}^{n+1}_{\xi}+\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\check{\psi}^{n+1-k}_{\xi}}\check{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}\right]\Big).

We turn to the
\varepsilon
-expansion of
\{\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}\}_{m}
, cf. (
1.26
): we write their expansions as

(1.32)

\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}\,\sim\,\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]}\sum_{n\geq 0}\varepsilon^{n}\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,m},

and it remains to write PDEs to characterize the coefficients
\{\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
.
For
m=0
, inserting (
1.31
) and separating powers of
\varepsilon
in the defining equation

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{0}=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1,

we find that the maps
\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,0}:Q\to\mathbb{C}
are defined inductively for all
n\geq 0
by

(1.33)

-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla\check{\zeta}^{n,0}_{\xi}=\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}i\xi\check{\zeta}^{n-1,0}_{\xi})+i\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\big(\nabla\check{\zeta}^{n-1,0}_{\xi}+i\xi\check{\zeta}^{n-2,0}_{\xi}\big)-\check{\psi}^{n+1}_{\xi}+\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\check{\psi}^{n+1-k}_{\xi}}\check{\psi}^{k}_{\xi}\right],

with nontrivial integration constant fixed as

(1.34)

\mathbb{E}\big[\check{\zeta}^{n,0}_{\xi}\big]\,:=\,-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{l=2}^{k+2}(\overline{\check{\lambda}^{l}_{\xi}/\check{\lambda}^{2}_{\xi}})\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\check{\psi}^{k+2-l}_{\xi}}\check{\zeta}^{n-k,0}_{\xi}\right].

(Recall again that by convention we implicitly set
\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,0}\equiv 0
for
n<0
.)
Again, these objects are well-defined by induction and the Fredholm alternative since our choice (
1.34
) precisely ensures that the right-hand side of (
1.33
) has vanishing periodic average; cf. Section
2.1
.
Next, we argue iteratively for
m\geq 1
:
starting from the defining equation

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m}=\Psi_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{m-1},

we find that the maps
\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,m}:Q\to\mathbb{C}
are defined inductively for all
n\geq 0
by

(1.35)

-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla\check{\zeta}^{n,m}_{\xi}\,=\,\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}i\xi\check{\zeta}^{n-1,m}_{\xi})+i\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\big(\nabla\check{\zeta}^{n-1,m}_{\xi}+i\xi\check{\zeta}^{n-2,m}_{\xi}\big)+\check{\zeta}^{n,m-1}_{\xi},

with nontrivial integration constant fixed as

(1.36)

\mathbb{E}\big[\check{\zeta}^{n,m}_{\xi}\big]\,:=\,-(\overline{1/\check{\lambda}^{2}_{\xi}})\sum_{k=0}^{n+2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\check{\psi}^{n+2-k}_{\xi}}\check{\zeta}^{k,m-1}_{\xi}\right]-{\sum_{k=2}^{n}}\sum_{l=2}^{k+1}(\overline{\check{\lambda}^{l}_{\xi}/\check{\lambda}^{2}_{\xi}})\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\check{\psi}^{k+1-l}_{\xi}}\check{\zeta}^{n-k,m}_{\xi}\right].

Putting all this together, using expansions (
1.28
) and (
1.32
), and extracting the polynomial
\xi
-dependence of the coefficients in the notation,

\check{\psi}_{\xi}^{n}\,=\,\psi^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes n},\qquad\check{\lambda}_{\xi}^{n+1}\,=\,\xi\cdot(\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes(n-1)})\xi,\qquad\check{\zeta}_{\xi}^{n,m}\,=\,\zeta^{n,m}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes(n+1)},

the spectral formula (
1.25
) appears to be precisely equivalent to the spectral two-scale ansatz (
1.18
),
with Fourier multiplier
\gamma
given by

\gamma(\xi)\,:=\,\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\xi}|^{2}\right]^{-1},

and with
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the associated formal homogenized equation, cf. (
1.5
),

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}+\sum_{n\geq 2}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\odot(\varepsilon\nabla)^{n-1}\Big)\nabla\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\sim\,f,

where dispersive corrections correspond to derivatives of the fibered ground states
\{\lambda_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
at
\xi=0
.
We have also derived the PDE hierarchies defining correctors
\{\psi^{n},\zeta^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
, cf. (
1.29
), (
1.33
), and (
1.35
).
For the proof of Theorems
1
and
3
, we replace infinite series by finite sums and show by PDE techniques that these are still good approximations for the wave flow.
In the random setting, just as in the elliptic case
[
18
,
4
,
21
,
15
]
, we can only solve a finite number of the above corrector equations, which is why a homogenized description is only obtained up to some maximal timescale and accuracy, depending both on space dimension and on mixing properties of the coefficient field.

1.5.
Geometric approach and hyperbolic two-scale expansion

Instead of starting from the above spectral analysis,
another way to describe oscillations of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
) is to appeal more directly to two-scale expansion techniques
[
6
]
and rather postulate the following natural hyperbolic two-scale ansatz,

(1.37)

u_{\varepsilon}\,\sim\,H^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}]\,:=\,\sum_{n,m\geq 0}\varepsilon^{n+m}\phi^{n,m}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\odot\nabla^{n}\partial_{t}^{m}\bar{v}_{\varepsilon},

where time and space play essentially symmetric roles and where
\bar{v}_{\varepsilon}
should satisfy some effective (constant-coefficient) hyperbolic equation.
Inserting this ansatz into the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
) and separating powers of
\varepsilon
, we are led to defining hyperbolic correctors as Allaire, Lamacz, and Rauch in
[
3
, Definition 2.2]
.
These correctors can be viewed as a refinement of usual elliptic correctors: for all
n\geq 0
, the
n
th-order hyperbolic corrector
\phi^{n,0}
and homogenized tensor
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,0}
defined in Section
3
indeed coincide with their elliptic counterparts
[
6
,
21
,
15
]
.

As in the elliptic setting, hyperbolic correctors have a natural geometric interpretation.
We focus on the periodic case to simplify the presentation.
The first corrector
\phi^{1,0}
, which is the same as in the elliptic setting, is defined to correct Euclidean coordinates
x\mapsto x_{i}
into
\boldsymbol{a}
-harmonic ones
x\mapsto x_{i}+\phi_{i}^{1,0}(x)
: indeed,
\phi_{i}^{1,0}
is the unique periodic solution of

-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla\phi_{i}^{1,0}+e_{i})=0,

with
\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{1,0}\right]=0
.
The corresponding two-scale expansion
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}[\bar{v}]\,:=\,\bar{v}+\varepsilon\phi_{i}^{1,0}(\frac{\cdot}{\varepsilon})\nabla_{i}\bar{v}
is then viewed as an intrinsic Taylor expansion of the limiting profile
\bar{v}
in terms of
\boldsymbol{a}
-harmonic coordinates.
In order to describe oscillations with finer accuracy, higher-order correctors are iteratively defined to correct higher-order polynomials and make them adapted to the heterogeneous wave operator. More precisely, higher-order correctors
\{\phi^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
are defined in such a way that, for any polynomial
{\bar{p}}
in space-time variables
x,t
, the corrected polynomial

(1.38)

H^{\infty}[{\bar{p}}]\,:=\,\sum_{n,m\geq 0}\phi^{n,m}\odot\nabla^{n}\partial_{t}^{m}{\bar{p}}

captures oscillations of the heterogeneous wave operator in the sense that

(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla)H^{\infty}[{\bar{p}}]

has no periodic oscillations any longer. In that case, this quantity can automatically be written as

(1.39)

(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla)H^{\infty}[{\bar{p}}]\,=\,\bigg(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{m\geq 0}\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\odot\nabla^{n-1}\partial_{t}^{m}\Big)\nabla\bigg){\bar{p}},

for some suitable family
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
of constant tensors; see Proposition
3.6
. This reflects the fact that on large scales the heterogeneous constitutive relation
\nabla u\mapsto\boldsymbol{a}\nabla u
is replaced by the effective relation
\nabla\bar{u}\mapsto\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\nabla\bar{u}
at leading order, while additional corrective terms must be included when looking for finer accuracy,

(1.40)

\nabla\bar{u}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mapsto\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{m\geq 0}\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}^{n,m}\odot\nabla^{n-1}\partial_{t}^{m}\Big)\nabla\bar{u}.

The difference with the elliptic setting is that in the present hyperbolic setting both space and time variables need to be corrected alike.
Comparing to the spectral approach, note that the presence of mixed space-time derivatives in the resulting homogenized equation (
1.10
) leads to additional well-posedness issues: naïve notions of solution display a secular growth, which was first avoided in
[
3
]
as explained at the end of Section
1.3
.

1.6.
Ill-prepared data

Up to now, we have focused on well-prepared data, or equivalently, on ancient solutions of the heterogeneous wave equation (
1.2
).
We now briefly discuss the effect of ill-prepared data by means of Floquet–Bloch theory, which provides further insight on the claims of Remark
1.4
.
For that purpose, as in Proposition
1.5
, we assume that the coefficient field
\boldsymbol{a}
is periodic and that the impulse
f
has compactly supported spatial Fourier transform: in this setting, for
\varepsilon
small enough, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}
has discrete spectrum and its lowest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi}
is simple for all
\xi
in the Fourier support of
f
.
We then show that, if initial data do not fit spatial oscillations of the ground state,
their projection on higher modes propagates and maintains forever, leading to an
O(\varepsilon)
contribution that consists of a superposition of typically incommensurate time oscillations with
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
frequency. This almost-periodic structure prohibits any approximate description by a two-scale expansion beyond accuracy
O(\varepsilon)
.
More precisely, we consider the initial-value problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\tfrac{\cdot}{\varepsilon}+q)\nabla\big)z_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)=0,&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
z_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)|_{t=0}=u^{\circ},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$},\\
\partial_{t}z_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)|_{t=0}=v^{\circ},&\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$}.\end{array}\right.

By Floquet–Bloch theory, arguing as for (
1.27
), we now get

z_{\varepsilon}(x,q)\\
=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}(\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}1)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\bigg(\cos\big(t(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)\,\hat{u}^{\circ}(\xi)+\frac{\sin\big(t(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\,\hat{v}^{\circ}(\xi)\bigg)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+r_{\varepsilon}(x,q),

where the remainder
r_{\varepsilon}
contains all the effects of initial ill-preparedness,

r_{\varepsilon}(x,q)\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\Big(\cos\big(t(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1\Big)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\hat{u}^{\circ}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\bigg(\frac{\sin\big(t(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\pi_{\varepsilon\xi}^{\bot}1\bigg)(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\,\hat{v}^{\circ}(\xi)\,d^{*}\xi.

Denoting by
\{\nu_{\varepsilon\xi}^{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
the non-decreasing sequence of eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon\xi}
on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(Q)
(repeated according to multiplicity, with
\nu_{\varepsilon\xi}^{1}>\nu_{\varepsilon\xi}^{0}=\lambda_{\varepsilon\xi}
), and denoting by
\{\gamma_{\varepsilon\xi}^{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
a corresponding sequence of normalized eigenfunctions, the above remainder can be written as

r_{\varepsilon}(x,q)\\
=\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\kappa^{n}_{\varepsilon\xi}\gamma_{\varepsilon\xi}^{n}(\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}+q)\bigg(\cos\big(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon}(\nu_{\varepsilon\xi}^{n})^{1/2}\big)\hat{u}^{\circ}(\xi)+\frac{\varepsilon\sin\big(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}(\nu^{n}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}\big)}{(\nu^{n}_{\varepsilon\xi})^{1/2}}\,\hat{v}^{\circ}(\xi)\bigg)\,d^{*}\xi,

in terms of
\kappa^{n}_{\varepsilon\xi}:=\mathbb{E}\big[\gamma_{\varepsilon\xi}^{n}\big]=O(\varepsilon\xi)
.
As claimed, this shows that the remainder
r_{\varepsilon}
is of order
O(\varepsilon)
and oscillates both in space and time with
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
frequency. Moreover, at a fixed Fourier mode
\xi
, the time dependence is (typically) almost-periodic, which prohibits any approximate description by means of a two-scale expansion beyond accuracy
O(\varepsilon)
.

As explained in Remark
1.4
, these complicated oscillations are naturally removed by taking time averages. In terms of the above remainder
r_{\varepsilon}
, this amounts to noting that, given
\theta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, we formally have for all
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta(t-s)\cos(\tfrac{s}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1/2})\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{2k}{(-1)^{k+1}}\lambda^{-k-1}\theta^{(2k+1)}(t),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta(t-s)\frac{\varepsilon\sin(\tfrac{s}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1/2})}{\lambda^{1/2}}\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{2k}{(-1)^{k+1}}\lambda^{-k-1}\theta^{(2k)}(t),

so that the above flow decomposition is precisely turned into an expansion of the form (
1.25
) for the time-averaged flow (
1
).

2.
Spectral approach and two-scale expansion

This section is devoted to the definition of spectral correctors and to the proof of Theorems
1
and
3
and of Corollary
1
, including the well-posedness of the formal homogenized equation (
1.5
).

2.1.
Definition of spectral correctors

We start by recalling the definition of the spectral correctors
\{\psi^{n}\}_{n}
and homogenized tensors
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\}_{n}
, as first introduced in
[
5
, Definition 2.1]
and motivated in Section
1.4
, cf. (
1.29
). We further introduce Fourier multipliers
\{\gamma_{\ell}\}_{\ell}
, which are proxies for the factor
\mathbb{E}\left[|\psi_{\varepsilon\xi}|^{2}\right]^{-1}
in (
1.30
), (
1.31
), and (
1.32
).

Definition 2.1

(Spectral correctors)
.

For all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define
\{\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi},\check{\lambda}^{n}_{\xi}\}_{n\geq 0}
inductively via
\check{\psi}^{0}_{\xi}=1
,
\check{\lambda}^{0}_{\xi}=0
, and for all
n\geq 1
we define
\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{per}}(Q)
as the periodic scalar field that has vanishing average
\mathbb{E}\big[\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}\big]=0
and satisfies

(2.1)

-\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\nabla\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi}=\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi})+i\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi}+i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-2}_{\xi})+\sum_{k=2}^{n}\check{\lambda}_{\xi}^{k}\check{\psi}_{\xi}^{n-k},

where we have defined

(2.2)

\check{\lambda}^{n}_{\xi}=-\mathbb{E}\left[i\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla\check{\psi}^{n-1}_{\xi}+i\xi\check{\psi}^{n-2}_{\xi})\right],

recalling that we implicitly set
\check{\psi}_{\xi}^{n}\equiv 0
for
n<0
for notational convenience.
Factoring out powers of
i\xi
in the above, we may then define the real-valued symmetric tensor fields
\{\psi^{n}\}_{n}
and symmetric tensors
\{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\}_{n}
via

(2.3)

\psi^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes n}=\check{\psi}^{n}_{\xi},\qquad\xi\cdot(\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes(n-1)})\xi=\check{\lambda}^{n+1}_{\xi}.

We shall also use the notation
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{1}
as the latter coincides with the homogenized coefficient for the associated elliptic equation.
Next, for all
\ell\geq 1
, we can define the following Fourier multiplier,

(2.4)

\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)\,:=\,\mathbb{E}\big[\textstyle|\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\psi^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes n}|^{2}\big]^{-1}.\qed

Remarks 2.2
.

\bullet

As the choice (
2.2
) precisely ensures that the right-hand side of (
2.1
) has vanishing average, an iterative use of the Poincaré inequality on the unit cell
Q
easily yields the following estimates: for all
n
,

(2.5)

|\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}|+\|\psi^{n}\|_{H^{1}(Q)}\leq C^{n}.

This ensures in particular the well-posedness of equations (
2.1
) & (
2.2
).

\bullet

Since by definition we have

\mathbb{E}\big[\textstyle\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\psi^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes n}\big]=1,

Jensen’s inequality yields

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\big[|\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\psi^{n}\odot(i\xi)^{\otimes n}|^{2}\big]\,\geq\,1,

which ensures that the definition (
2.4
) of the Fourier multiplier
\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)
indeed makes sense and satisfies
\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)\leq 1
for all
\xi
. In addition, in view of (
2.5
), we can expand, for
|\xi|\ll 1
small enough,

(2.6)

\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)=1+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\gamma_{\ell}^{k}\odot\xi^{\otimes k},

for some coefficients
\{\gamma_{\ell}^{k}\}_{k}
. In addition, this can be truncated to any order
n\geq 0
,

(2.7)

\Big|\gamma_{\ell}(\xi)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\gamma_{\ell}^{k}\odot\xi^{\otimes k}\Big|\,\leq\,(C|\xi|)^{n+1}.\qed

\bullet

The uniform ellipticity of
\boldsymbol{a}
, cf. (
1.1
), ensures that
\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}=\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{1}
is elliptic, and therefore we have
\check{\lambda}_{\xi}^{2}=\xi\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{a}}\xi\geq\lambda|\xi|^{2}
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

As a consequence of the self-adjointness of fibered operators
\{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
in Section
1.4
,
their first eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{\xi}\}_{\xi}
are real, hence, in view of (
1.28
) and (
2.3
), we deduce that
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}
must vanish for all
n
even. Equivalently,
\check{\lambda}_{\xi}^{n}
vanishes for
n
odd. This can alternatively be proven by a direct computation starting from definition (
2.2
), cf.
[
5
, Proposition 1]
; a similar (more involved) argument will be provided in Proposition
3.5
, so we skip the detail for now.

Proposition 2.3
.

We have
\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{n}=0
for all
n
even.
∎

As is common in the elliptic setting, it is useful to further introduce suitable flux correctors, which will allow us to refine error estimates by directly exploiting cancellations due to fluxes having vanishing average.

Definition 2.4

(Spectral flux correctors and auxiliary correctors)
.

For
n\geq 0
, we define the spectral flux correctors
\sigma^{n}:=(\sigma^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}})_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\leq d}
by

\sigma_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}^{n}=(\nabla\Phi_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}^{n})^{T},

where
\Phi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{per}}(Q)^{d}
is the periodic vector field that satisfies
\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}^{n}\big]=0
and

-\triangle(\Phi_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}^{n})_{i_{n+1}}\,=\,e_{i_{n+1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}(\nabla\psi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}+\psi^{n-1}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}}e_{i_{n}})-\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}(e_{i_{k}}\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}^{k-1}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k-2}}e_{i_{k-1}})\psi^{n+1-k}_{i_{k+1}\ldots i_{n+1}}.

For
n\geq 2
, we also define the auxiliary correctors
\rho^{n}:=(\rho^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}})_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\leq d}
by

\rho^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}=\nabla_{i_{n}}\Psi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}},

where
\Psi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}}\in\lx@nobreakspace H^{1}_{\operatorname{per}}(Q)
is the periodic vector field that satisfies
\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}}\big]=0
and

-\triangle\Psi^{n}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}}=\psi^{n-1}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n-1}}.\qed

Next, we turn to the definition of the correctors
\{\zeta^{n,m}\}_{n,m}
, as motivated in Section
1.4
, cf. (
1.33
) and (
1.35
), and we start with the case
m=0
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For all
n\geq 0
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we recursively define
